1f:["$","p",null,{"className":"text-xs text-ink-500","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"font-mono","children":"−2"}]," strongly disagree … ",["$","span",null,{"className":"font-mono","children":"+2"}]," ","strongly agree. A dash means we have no code we can stand behind for that party on that statement — either the party published nothing we could code, or our two independent coders split and the row is waiting on a blind tie-break. Either way it is recorded as ",["$","span",null,{"className":"font-mono","children":"null"}],", never guessed and never scored as neutral; the source list below carries the reasoning."]}]
20:["$","p",null,{"className":"text-sm text-ink-700","children":["In plain terms: when the two coders disagree, we do not split the difference. The cell is published as ",["$","span",null,{"className":"font-mono","children":"null"}]," — a blank, not a zero — until a third, blind pass or a human tie-break settles it. Why so strict? A guessed code would put words in a party’s mouth, and counting silence as ",["$","span",null,{"className":"font-mono","children":"0"}]," ","would quietly invent a neutral position the party never took. A ",["$","span",null,{"className":"font-mono","children":"null"}]," ","is left out of that party’s averages and out of the alignment math entirely, so a split can never nudge a score in anyone’s favour. Every split and its resolution stays in the public record. This is one of the published rules of the"," ",["$","$L2",null,{"className":"text-accent underline underline-offset-2","href":"/methodology/#neutrality-protocol","children":"neutrality protocol"}],"."]}]
